This is the strongest case yet across all mobile units for closing this gap before Unit 18 builds further on top.
- **The `401`-triggers-`logout()` pattern was repeated a third time (Location/device/punch calls) rather than centralized** — the same open question raised in Unit 16 still stands, now with three independent call sites (`device.tsx`, and this unit's `attendance.tsx` for both device-status and punch calls) each repeating the identical `if (error instanceof ApiError && error.status === 401) { await logout(); return; }` block. Worth centralizing into `authorizedRequest` itself (e.g. a registered callback the auth context installs) before a fourth screen repeats it again in Unit 18.
- **The 100-meter accuracy threshold and `Location.Accuracy.High` request level are both hardcoded, not policy-configurable** — the task explicitly suggested "a simple threshold like 100 meters" for this foundation unit, so this is intentional, but a real company policy might want this tunable per work-location type (office vs. large warehouse/project site) once Unit 8's backend geofence rules and this client-side pre-check need to agree more precisely.
- **`mockLocationDetected` is Android-only** (`LocationObject.mocked` is documented by `expo-location` itself as `@platform android`) — on iOS this always reports `false` regardless of whether the location is genuinely real, since iOS has no equivalent public API for mock-location detection via Expo. This is disclosed to the backend as-is (a `false` fraud signal that means "not detected," not "confirmed genuine") and the backend's own fraud engine (Unit 8) does not currently weight this signal differently by platform — worth flagging to whoever tunes fraud scoring in a future unit.
- **The Device Status and Location Status cards duplicate a fetch-and-render shape already used on the Device screen** (Unit 16) — reusing `MOBILE_DEVICE_STATUS_COPY` closes most of the duplication, but the actual `getDeviceIdentifier()` + `getMyMobileDevices()` fetch/state-management logic is still independently reimplemented on both screens. A shared `useDeviceRegistrationStatus()` hook could remove this if a third screen ever needs the same status, but two occurrences alone didn't yet justify the abstraction (matching this project's own "reused by 2+" threshold, which is borderline here, not clearly crossed).

### Unit 18 (Mobile Attendance History and Status Screen) Open Questions

- **No Android emulator, iOS simulator, or physical device was available in this environment** — the same gap carried forward from Units 15–17, now 4 units deep. Full backend contract verification was performed live via curl (register/approve/punch/validate/calculate/list-me for both punches and summaries all confirmed), and both Android/iOS bundles were confirmed to compile with the new screen sections, but no one has actually pulled-to-refresh or scrolled through a real Today/History card on a real device yet.
- **The `pnpm-workspace.yaml` `packageExtensions` fix for `@hookform/resolvers` addresses this one specific package** — the same underlying pattern (a third-party package relying on phantom-hoisted `zod` rather than a declared dependency) could exist in other dependencies too, undetected until the next unrelated lockfile change happens to break it for a different app. Worth a repo-wide audit of `pnpm why zod` across all workspace packages before this class of surprise recurs a third time.
- **The web/backend dev-server crash caused by this unit's own `pnpm install` is a real, demonstrated risk for every future unit that adds a new dependency to any one app** — installing a package into `apps/mobile` measurably disrupted already-running `apps/api`/`apps/web` dev servers, because pnpm's dependency resolution is one shared graph across the whole monorepo. Future units should expect to restart other apps' dev servers after any `pnpm add`/`pnpm install`, not just the app being worked on.
- **The Today Status card and Summary History section both derive "today" from the same 15-row summaries fetch, capped at `limit: 15`** — if an employee's summary history ever has a gap (e.g. they were on leave and no summary was calculated for several weeks) such that today's entry would be outside the most recent 15 rows, this derivation would incorrectly show "no summary yet" even though one exists further back. This can't actually happen in practice (today's summary, if calculated, is always the newest row given the `workDate: desc` ordering), but the assumption is worth re-checking if the ordering or scope of this fetch ever changes in a future unit.
- **No shift/schedule display exists yet** — the Today card shows worked/late/early/overtime minutes but not what shift the employee was scheduled for, since no shift-assignment API exists in the mobile client yet (this is explicitly Unit 1's schema, exposed nowhere in `apps/mobile` so far). `ui-context.md`'s "Employee Mobile Home... Today shift" layout guidance is only partially satisfied by this unit; a future unit should decide whether shift display belongs on this screen or a separate one.

### Unit 18.1 (Mobile Navigation and Logout UX Fix) Open Questions

- **The biggest open question from this unit, by far**: `apps/mobile`'s actual resolved dependency set has drifted from what every one of Units 15–18's progress-tracker entries describes as "SDK 57" (`expo@^57.0.4`, `expo-router@~57.0.4`, `react-native@0.86.0`) down to what now resolves as the tooling's own idea of "correct" — `expo@^54.0.35`, `expo-router@~6.0.24`, `react-native@0.81.5`. This was discovered, investigated, and the corrected set was verified safe to keep (typecheck/lint/bundle-compile all still pass for every existing screen), but the root *why* was never fully nailed down — whether "57" was ever a real, stable, publicly-released Expo SDK version at the time of Unit 15's install, or a transient/mistaken registry state, is unknown from inside this environment (no live web access to check Expo's actual release history). **Before Unit 19 or any future mobile unit installs a new dependency, re-run `npx expo install --check` first** to see the currently-recommended state up front, rather than discovering another silent correction mid-unit.
- **No Android emulator, iOS simulator, or physical device was available in this environment** — the same gap carried forward from Units 15–18, now 6 units deep (counting this as 18.1), and the most consequentially relevant instance yet: this entire unit exists *because* a real phone screenshot caught a bug that no amount of `tsc`/`eslint`/bundle-compile verification ever could have (a completely unreachable screen). This is no longer a theoretical risk to flag — it has now concretely caused a real, shipped defect. Strongly recommend securing real device/emulator access before Unit 19 adds a correction-request form, which will have considerably more interactive surface (multi-field forms, conditional fields) than anything shipped so far.
- **Tab bar visual polish was not addressed beyond icons + labels + basic color tokens** — no badge counts (e.g. a red dot on Attendance if a punch was just rejected), no haptic feedback on tab press, no "you are here" animation beyond Expo Router's own defaults. These are reasonable future polish candidates once real device feedback exists to justify them, not addressed here since the task's scope was explicitly "make navigation obvious," not "polish navigation."
- **The Home screen's disabled "Corrections" card has no explanation of *when* it will be available** — it just says "Coming soon." Once Unit 19 ships, this card should either become a real link or be updated with a more specific message; left as a plain placeholder for now since building Unit 19 itself is explicitly out of scope for this unit.

### Unit 19 (Mobile Attendance Correction Request Screen) Open Questions

- **No Android emulator, iOS simulator, or physical device was available in this environment** — the same gap carried forward from Units 15–18.1, now 7 units deep, and the strongest case yet for closing it: this is the most interactive mobile screen shipped so far (a multi-field form with conditionally-shown fields driven by a chip selector, not just a fetch-and-display screen), and chip-selection/conditional-field-visibility UX has never been exercised on real touch hardware. Full backend contract verification was performed live via curl (request/approve/reject/employeeId-injection/no-employee all confirmed) and both bundles compile, but no one has actually tapped a correction-type chip on a real device yet.
- **The `targetPunchId` field was added to the mobile form even though the task's own literal spec omitted it** (see Completed notes) — this was a judgment call to keep `WRONG_IN`/`WRONG_OUT` correction types functional rather than silently 400ing, mirroring the same call made for the web form in Unit 14. Worth confirming with a real HR/employee user whether typing a raw punch ID by hand is acceptable UX, or whether a future polish pass should let the employee pick from their own recent punches (`GET /attendance-punches/me`) instead of typing an ID blind.
- **The `401`-triggers-`logout()` pattern was repeated a fourth time** (this screen's list-load and submit calls) rather than centralized — the same open question raised in Units 16–18 still stands, now with four independent call sites across `device.tsx`, `attendance.tsx`, and this unit's `corrections.tsx`. Worth centralizing into `authorizedRequest` itself before a fifth screen repeats it again.
- **Requested Punch Time is a plain ISO-8601 text input** (e.g. `2026-07-15T08:00:00.000Z`), not a native date/time picker — acceptable per the task's explicit "simple date/time input is acceptable for this unit" allowance and this project's established "no heavy libraries unless clearly justified" pattern, but a real employee typing a raw ISO timestamp by hand is a rough UX; a native date/time picker (e.g. `@react-native-community/datetimepicker`) is a reasonable future polish candidate once real device feedback confirms it's needed.

### Unit 19.2 (Employee User Account Provisioning + Password Change Reminder) Open Questions

- **`HR_MANAGER` was deliberately left without `employees.createUserAccount`/`users.resetPassword`**, per the task's own literal role-mapping instruction, even though `HR_MANAGER` already holds broader employee-management permissions (`employees.disable`, `users.create`/`users.update`/`users.disable`) than `HR_ADMIN` in the existing Unit 3 seed. This is a real, deliberate policy asymmetry now encoded in `rbac.constants.ts` — confirm with actual HR org-chart/approval-chain policy whether this is intentional (e.g., account provisioning is meant to be a narrower, more junior HR_ADMIN-only responsibility) or was simply an oversight in how the task was worded, before this drifts further from real company policy.
- **"Revoke except current session" was not implemented for either `change-password` or `reset-password`** — both revoke *every* active refresh token for the user, not just other sessions, because `AccessTokenPayload` carries no `jti`/session identifier tying it back to a specific `RefreshToken` row. This means a user who changes their own password mid-session on web will also be logged out of their own mobile session (and vice versa) — arguably fine from a security posture (force re-auth everywhere after a credential change) but worth deciding explicitly rather than leaving as an accidental side effect. A future improvement: thread a shared session/`jti` through both the access and refresh token payloads so "except current" becomes cheaply derivable.
- **The reminder's "Later" dismissal is in-memory only on both web and mobile**, not persisted via the new `passwordReminderDismissedAt` schema field — the field was added because the task allowed adding schema fields "if useful," but this unit's actual 2-minutes-or-next-login behavior doesn't need server-side persistence to satisfy the stated requirement (a fresh login already naturally resets in-memory state, since the whole authenticated app tree unmounts on logout). This means dismissing the reminder on one device/tab does **not** suppress it on another concurrent session for the same user — if that cross-device consistency is ever desired, `passwordReminderDismissedAt` is already in the schema and just needs a `PATCH`-style endpoint and read-path wiring; it does nothing today.
- **No 7-day grace-period enforcement exists yet**, exactly as scoped ("recommended grace period: 7 days... but do not enforce grace period yet") — `temporaryPasswordIssuedAt` is stored and returned via `/auth/me`, which is precisely the data a future unit would need to compute "days since temp password issued" and force a change past some threshold, but no such check exists anywhere yet.
- **No Android emulator, iOS simulator, or physical device was available in this environment** — the same gap carried forward from Units 15–19, now 8 units deep. This unit's mobile change-password screen and reminder banner were verified via `tsc`/`eslint` passing and a live Metro bundle-compile grep for the new source strings, plus the shared backend contract being independently verified via curl — but no one has tapped "Change Password" or "Later" on a real device yet. Similarly, **no browser-automation tool was available for the web side either** — the Employees page's new Create Login/Reset Password/one-time-credential-reveal flow and the header/reminder Change Password modal were verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`next build` passing and the same curl-verified backend contract, but never actually clicked through in a real browser. This is now the single largest verification gap across the whole project (covering both web and mobile in the same unit) and should be closed before Unit 20 (payroll-adjacent, higher-stakes) begins.

### Unit 20 (Payroll Lock / Monthly Closing Backend) Open Questions

- **No web or mobile UI exists yet for any of this** — exactly as scoped ("no web UI, no mobile UI"), but it means every payroll-lock action (create/lock/unlock) is currently only reachable via direct API calls. Unit 21 (Web Payroll Lock / Monthly Closing Screen) is the immediate, already-recommended next step to close this.
- **The pre-existing `attendance.payroll.lock`/`attendance.payroll.unlock` permissions (from Unit 3) are now genuinely redundant with the new `payrollLocks.*` set** but were deliberately left in place rather than removed, since removing them wasn't in this unit's scope and nothing currently reads them for a different purpose. Worth a deliberate cleanup pass in a future unit — either repurpose them for something distinct (e.g., a future payroll *export* permission) or formally deprecate/remove them, rather than carrying two parallel permission names for the same capability indefinitely.
- **`summaryCount` and `affectedSummaryCount` can theoretically diverge** (see Completed notes) if a summary in range were ever locked outside the normal lock flow — this can't happen under the current overlap-prevention invariant, but if a future unit ever allows manually setting `lockedAt` directly (bypassing `PayrollLocksService`), this assumption should be re-checked.
- **No automatic/scheduled monthly closing exists** — periods must be created and locked manually via the API for now, matching the task's "backend foundation only" framing. A future unit could add a scheduled job (BullMQ, per `architecture.md`'s cache/queue layer) that proposes a period automatically at month-end, but nothing like that was built here.
- **Overlap prevention only guards against a *second* active period covering the same dates — it does not validate the *content* of a period** (e.g., a payroll period spanning 400 days, or one that starts before the company's founding date). The task called this out as "date range reasonable" under validation but didn't specify a concrete bound; no maximum-span check was added, since no concrete policy number exists yet. Worth flagging to whoever defines the real monthly-closing policy in Unit 21 or later.

### Unit 19.3 (Employee Web/Mobile Experience Alignment) Open Questions

- **No browser-automation tool was available to click through the web changes**, and no Android emulator/iOS simulator/physical device was available for the mobile fix — the same disclosed gap carried from every prior frontend unit. Web verification relied on `tsc`/`eslint`/`next build` passing plus a live curl-verified backend contract; mobile verification relied on the same plus a fresh bundle-compile grep confirming exactly 5 `tabBarLabel` entries. Neither was actually clicked through by a human or automated browser/device. This is now the single most consequential instance of this recurring gap, since the reported bug (an empty employee sidebar, a broken extra tab) was itself only caught by a real screenshot, not by any of this project's automated checks — reinforcing the standing recommendation to close this gap before further UI units.
- **`isAdminUser()` treats "any admin-nav permission" as the entire admin/employee boundary** — this correctly fixes the reported `EMPLOYEE`-role case, but means a hypothetical future role holding, say, only `audit.read` (and nothing else admin-shaped) would see the *full* existing admin sidebar filtered down to just "Audit Logs" + "Dashboard," not a hybrid or the new employee nav. This matches the task's own literal instruction ("if user has admin permissions, show admin navigation as today") and is a reasonable default, but worth revisiting once more granular roles exist that might want a blended nav.
- **The new employee web pages are intentionally lightweight** (per the task's own "lightweight" framing) — no pagination controls, no date-range filters, no device management actions beyond a status display. If real employee usage shows a need for these (e.g., paging through more than the fetched 15–20 rows), a follow-up unit should add them rather than this one having spent time building filter UI nobody asked for yet.
- **The pre-existing `attendance.payroll.lock`/`attendance.payroll.unlock` vs. `payrollLocks.*` permission redundancy flagged in Unit 20 is unrelated and untouched here** — noted only to avoid it being mistaken for something this unit should have addressed.

### Unit 19.4 (Mobile Employee App UX Benchmark Alignment) Open Questions

- **No Android emulator, iOS simulator, or physical device was available in this environment** — the same gap carried from every mobile unit since Unit 15, now the most consequential instance yet: this unit introduced entirely new gesture surfaces (a `Modal`-based side-menu with tap-outside-to-close, a bottom sheet, a custom raised tab-bar button intercepting normal tab-press behavior) that have only ever been verified via `tsc`/`eslint` and a Metro bundle-compile-and-grep check, never a real tap. Strongly recommend closing this gap before this specific unit's navigation changes reach real users.
- **The center "+" tab button is a raised circular button integrated into the tab bar's own row layout, not a true floating FAB detached from it** — a deliberate, disclosed simplification per the task's own accepted fallback. If a genuinely floating center button (visually breaking out above the bar, independent of its background) is wanted later, it needs a custom tab-bar component (overriding `tabBarStyle`'s container entirely) rather than the current per-screen `tabBarButton` override, which is still constrained to the bar's own height/row.
- **The side menu uses a fade transition, not a horizontal slide-in** — the simplest correct implementation with RN's built-in `Modal`, explicitly chosen over building a custom `Animated.Value`-driven slide for a first version. If a slide-in feel is wanted, add an `Animated.timing`-driven `translateX` on the panel `View`, gated behind the same `visible` prop — no new dependency needed either way.
- **Home's avatar/greeting card is username-derived, not a real employee profile** — `/auth/me` still only returns `employeeId`, no `employeeCode`/`fullName`/`department`/`designation` (the same gap already flagged in Unit 19.2/19.3's Home-card work, now directly visible in the redesigned greeting card's initials and name). A future employee-profile-enrichment endpoint (returning these fields for the current user specifically, not the admin-gated full `/employees` list) would directly improve this card without any other change.
- **Leave balance cards, the Workflow screen's Draft count, and the Directory screen are all permanently "coming soon" placeholders with zero backend calls, by design** — none of them should be mistaken for partially-built features in a future unit; they're intentionally inert until a real leave/directory backend is scoped and built (explicitly out of scope for this unit and the two before it).

### Unit 19.5 (Web Employee Self-Service Benchmark Alignment) Open Questions

- **No browser-automation tool was available to click through the redesigned employee web experience** — the same disclosed gap carried from every web unit since Unit 11, and now the largest employee-facing web surface built so far (a grouped sidebar, a dropdown, a 7-section dashboard). Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`next build` passing plus a curl-verified backend contract and route-rendering checks, but never actually clicked through in a real browser. The task's own "prefer real manual browser check" recommendation stands unmet — strongly recommend a real click-through pass (including the profile dropdown's open/close/backdrop-click behavior, and the sidebar's group expansion reading) before this reaches real employees.
- **Work Location, Current Department, Employee Code, and full name are all still `—` on both the Dashboard and Profile page** — the same root cause flagged repeatedly since Unit 19.2 (`/auth/me` returns only `employeeId`, never a full employee record) now affects two more surfaces. A future self-service employee-profile endpoint (distinct from the admin-gated `GET /employees/:id` — this must remain an "own record only" endpoint, not a reuse of the admin one) would let all of these show real values across mobile Home, web Dashboard, and web/mobile Profile in one pass.
- **`ProfileDropdown` and the mobile `SideMenu`/`CreateActionSheet` (Unit 19.4) are independently implemented, not shared** — consistent with this project's established web/mobile code-separation convention (no cross-imports between `apps/web` and `apps/mobile`), but worth noting since they now serve near-identical purposes (profile access, change password, sign out) with separately-maintained copy. If the wording ever needs to change (e.g. "Sign Out" vs "Logout"), both platforms need the same edit made twice.
- **The employee sidebar's groups (Workflow/Time Management/Reports) are always expanded, with no collapse/expand interaction** — a deliberate simplification given each group only has 2–3 children; if the admin sidebar or a future employee nav ever grows deep enough to need collapsing, `EmployeeNavGroup` would need an `open`/`toggle` state added, which the current always-expanded rendering doesn't have.

### Unit 20.1 (Automated Attendance Validation and Daily Auto-Calculation) Open Questions

- **No mobile-source condition currently maps to `ACCEPTED_WITH_WARNING`** — the clean-case fallback for both mobile and device punches now produces `ACCEPTED` directly, so `ACCEPTED_WITH_WARNING` is defined in the schema and handled everywhere downstream (eligible for official-punch selection, same as `ACCEPTED`) but nothing currently emits it. A future non-blocking condition (e.g. GPS accuracy between two thresholds, or a minor-but-tolerable device-metadata mismatch) would be the natural first real use — flagged here so a future unit doesn't assume it's already wired to something.
- **The GPS accuracy threshold changed from an undocumented 50m dev-mode value to a documented, centralized 100m** (`MOBILE_GPS_ACCURACY_MAX_METERS`) — this is a real, deliberate policy loosening, not a bug fix, matching the mobile app's own existing client-side pre-check from Unit 17. If HR/company policy wants a stricter production threshold, this is the one constant to change.
- **Option B (employee-default work-location inference) is now load-bearing for real mobile geofence checking** — since the mobile app never sends `workLocationId` on real punches (Unit 17's own documented scope-out), nearly every real mobile punch's geofence check depends on `Employee.workLocationId` being correctly assigned. An employee with no assigned work location falls back to Option A (`PENDING_HR_REVIEW` + `WORK_LOCATION_NOT_ASSIGNED`), so HR onboarding data quality (every active employee having a real work location assigned) directly determines how much manual review volume this unit actually eliminates in practice.
- **Locked-period late punches remain `ACCEPTED`-status but never become official** — the raw punch is validated normally and stored with an honest accepted-family status (it *would* have been accepted if the date weren't locked), with `LOCKED_PERIOD_LATE_PUNCH` in `fraudFlags` as the review signal, rather than inventing a new rejection status for "otherwise-fine punch, wrong timing." No web/mobile UI currently surfaces this flag to HR — a future HR exception dashboard (this unit's flag vocabulary was designed with that dashboard in mind) is the natural place to do so.
- **The remaining payroll/salary calculation gap is unchanged and still fully out of scope** — this unit only automates punch validation and daily-summary recalculation; converting a `AttendanceDailySummary` into an actual payable amount (overtime rates, absence deductions, etc.) is Unit 20.2's job, not started here.
- **`ValidatePendingResult`'s field names were kept exactly as-is (`totalProcessed`/`accepted`/`rejected`/`pending`) rather than renamed to the task's suggested `processed`/`pendingReview`** — the two new fields (`summariesRecalculated`/`skippedLocked`) were added alongside, not instead. If a future unit wants the task's literal vocabulary, that would require a coordinated rename across the API DTO and `apps/web/lib/api/attendance-punches.ts` plus the consuming page — not attempted here since nothing required it.

### Unit 19.6 (Mobile Splash Screen and App Branding Polish) Open Questions

- **The correct Metro bundle-verification URL for this pnpm monorepo is not `/index.bundle?platform=...` — it must be read from the manifest each time**, not assumed from what worked in earlier units' write-ups. `metro.config.js`'s `watchFolders = [workspaceRoot]` makes the HTTP bundle endpoint resolve relative to the workspace root, so the real path is the fully-resolved `node_modules/.pnpm/expo-router@<hash>/node_modules/expo-router/entry.bundle?platform=<platform>&dev=true&hot=false&lazy=true&transform.engine=hermes&transform.routerRoot=app&unstable_transformProfile=hermes-stable`, obtained by fetching `GET /` (the manifest) and extracting the `<script src="...">` path. Every future mobile unit's bundle-compile verification step should fetch the manifest first rather than hardcoding `/index.bundle`.
- **No native splash-screen image asset was custom-designed for this unit** — the existing square, white-background `assets/icon.png` (the official RECAFCO logo, already used as the app icon) was reused as-is for the splash image too, per the task's own "if the logo file already exists, reuse it" instruction. If a dedicated, more minimal splash-only mark (e.g. just the wing icon without the full wordmark/tagline) is ever supplied, swapping the `expo-splash-screen` plugin's `image` path in `app.json` is the only change needed.
- **No emulator/physical device was available to visually confirm the native splash screen actually renders correctly** (background color, image centering, timing) — this is the same disclosed gap carried from every mobile unit since Unit 15. Verification was limited to confirming the Expo config plugin is correctly declared and that the JS-rendered branded loading screen (which *can* be verified via bundle content) compiles and contains the expected branding. A real device/emulator/EAS-build check is recommended before this reaches real employees, same as the outstanding recommendation already on file from Units 15–19.5.
- **No mobile dev server is left running as of the end of this unit** (see Session Notes) — unlike several prior mobile units that found and reused an already-running instance untouched, this unit's own dependency install killed the one that was running, and both of its own replacement instances were stopped after verification. The next unit touching `apps/mobile` will need to start a fresh `expo start` itself.

### Device Integration Questions

- What punching device brands/models are installed?
- Do devices support API, SDK, database sync, or CSV export?
- Are devices connected to LAN?
- Do devices store employee code matching HR employee ID?
- Do devices provide punch type IN/OUT, or only timestamp?
- How often should logs sync?
- Is there an existing database from the current device software?

### Deployment Questions

- Will production run on Linux server or Windows server?
- Is Docker allowed?
- Is the system LAN-only?
- Is VPN access required for managers/HR?
- What backup location is available?
- Is a standby server available?
- What uptime target should be promised internally?

## Architecture Decisions

### Decision: Use monorepo

Use pnpm workspace monorepo with `apps/web`, `apps/api`, `apps/mobile`, `packages/db`, `packages/shared`, and `packages/config`.

Reason: Shared HR statuses, permissions, validation schemas, and API types reduce mismatch across web, mobile, and backend.

### Decision: Use central attendance engine

All punch sources must flow into one raw punch table and one validation engine.

Reason: Employees may punch in by mobile and punch out by office device, or vice versa. Payroll-safe calculation requires one source of truth.

### Decision: Separate raw punches from summaries

Raw punches are immutable event records. Daily summaries are derived records.

Reason: Prevent salary disputes, support recalculation, preserve evidence, and improve auditability.

### Decision: Payroll closing lock

Monthly attendance summaries must be locked before payroll export.

Reason: Prevent silent changes after payroll processing.

### Decision: React Native mobile app

Use React Native for Android and iOS.

Reason: One TypeScript ecosystem with Next.js and NestJS, faster development, shared types, and sufficient support for GPS, camera, push notification, secure storage, and device binding.

### Decision: PostgreSQL as source of truth

Use PostgreSQL for HRMS data.

Reason: Attendance, employee, leave, approvals, and payroll support are relational and audit-heavy.

### Decision: Redis/BullMQ for heavy work

Use background jobs for reports, device imports, notifications, recalculations where needed.

Reason: Punch-in/out must remain fast and reliable.

### Decision: Prisma 6.19.3 (not the 7.x line) for `@recafco/db`

Pinned `prisma`/`@prisma/client` to `6.19.3` even though `7.x` is the current latest major on npm.

Reason: Prisma 7 changes the client generator (`prisma-client` vs. `prisma-client-js`), generally requires an explicit driver adapter (e.g. `@prisma/adapter-pg`) and a `prisma.config.ts`, and is ESM-only output. That's a bigger architectural surface than this foundation unit needs, and it can't be fully verified here without a live Postgres connection. Revisit the major-version choice as a deliberate decision (not a side effect) when the API app actually wires up the database connection.

### Decision: Device import idempotency via compound unique constraint

`AttendanceRawPunch` has `@@unique([attendanceDeviceId, sourceId])`.

Reason: `code-standards.md` requires unique constraints for device import deduplication. Postgres treats each `NULL` in a unique index as distinct, so mobile/web punches (`attendanceDeviceId` null) never collide with each other, while device-imported punches from the same device with the same external log id are rejected as duplicates at the database level, independent of the validation engine's own duplicate-detection logic.

### Decision: Root `.env` is the single source of truth for `DATABASE_URL`

`packages/db` does not duplicate a real `.env`; only `packages/db/.env.example` was added as a template, since Prisma CLI resolves `.env` relative to its own working directory, not the repo root, when run via `pnpm --filter`.

Reason: Avoid two copies of the same secret drifting out of sync. If `pnpm --filter @recafco/db db:generate/db:migrate/db:studio` needs `DATABASE_URL` locally, copy the root `.env` value into a new `packages/db/.env` (gitignored, not committed).

### Decision: `@recafco/db` (and future workspace libraries consumed by `apps/api`) must ship a real CommonJS build

Changed `packages/db` from `"type": "module"` + a `main` pointing directly at TypeScript source, to a proper `tsc`-compiled CommonJS `dist/` output.

Reason: `apps/api` is a NestJS app compiled with `tsc` to CommonJS and run with plain `node dist/main.js` in production (and via `nest start --watch` in dev, which also runs compiled JS, not `ts-node`). A CommonJS `require()` cannot load a raw `.ts` file. This wasn't visible in Unit 1 because nothing outside `packages/db` itself was importing it yet. `packages/shared` has the same latent problem and needs the identical fix before anything CommonJS-based imports it (see Unit 2 open questions).

### Decision: `DatabaseModule` is `@Global()`, wraps the existing `@recafco/db` singleton, and never throws on startup

`DatabaseService.onModuleInit()` calls `prisma.$connect()` but only logs a warning if it fails; it does not rethrow.

Reason: The hard requirement is that `GET /health` must work even if the database is down. If a failed `$connect()` threw during `onModuleInit`, Nest would abort the entire bootstrap and no endpoint — including `/health` — would ever become reachable. Swallowing the connect error (and letting Prisma reconnect lazily on the next query, which is how `/health/db` observes real connectivity) keeps the liveness check independent of the readiness check, which is the standard health-check pattern. Marked `@Global()` since nearly every future domain module (employees, attendance, leave, etc.) will need database access, and re-importing `DatabaseModule` in every feature module would be pure boilerplate.

### Decision: env validation is local to `apps/api` for now, not in `packages/config`

Reason: `packages/config` is currently an empty folder with no package.json. Standing up a full shared env-schema package for a single `zod` object with three fields would be speculative infrastructure ahead of actual need. Revisit when a second app needs typed env validation (see open questions).

### Decision: `argon2` for password hashing (not `bcryptjs`)

Tested `argon2` directly on this Windows machine (a real hash/verify round-trip, not just `npm install` succeeding) before deciding — it installed via a prebuilt native binary with zero build-tool friction, so used it per the task's stated preference order.

Reason: argon2id is the stronger, more modern choice recommended by current password-hashing guidance, and there was no actual Windows compatibility problem to justify the bcryptjs fallback.

### Decision: refresh tokens are real JWTs, but the database (via a SHA-256 hash) is the actual source of truth

A refresh token is a signed JWT (`{ sub, jti }`, signed with `JWT_REFRESH_SECRET`), but its validity is decided by looking up `refresh_tokens.tokenHash` (a hash of the whole token string) in the database — not by trusting the JWT's own `exp` claim alone. Rotated on every use: the old row is marked `revokedAt`, a new row + token pair is issued.

Reason: The task's env-var design (`JWT_REFRESH_SECRET`, `JWT_REFRESH_EXPIRES_IN`) implies refresh tokens should be signed JWTs like access tokens, but a DB-backed opaque-credential model is what actually allows "verify hashed stored refresh token" (as the task's endpoint spec requires) and real revocation (logout, admin-forced logout later). Combining both — JWT signature as a second independent check, DB row as the authoritative one — gets the benefits of each without a real tradeoff. Raw tokens are never persisted, only their hash.

### Decision: access-token JWT payload embeds roles and permissions

`AccessTokenPayload` carries `roles: string[]` and `permissions: string[]` directly, so `PermissionsGuard` reads them from the verified JWT (via Passport's `JwtStrategy`) with no per-request database query.

Reason: Punch/attendance-adjacent endpoints must stay fast per `architecture.md`'s reliability requirements, and re-resolving roles→permissions via three-table joins on every guarded request would add avoidable latency at scale (2000+ employees). The tradeoff — a permission change doesn't take effect until the user's next token refresh (≤15 minutes by default) — is acceptable for an internal HRMS and is the standard JWT-RBAC pattern. `GET /auth/me` still does a live DB read, so a user (or support staff) can always see their *current* actual permissions on demand, independent of what's cached in their current access token.

### Decision: `Role`/`Permission` are database rows with a `code` string, not Prisma enums

Reason: The task modeled them as tables (with `isSystemRole`/`description` fields), which only makes sense if they're meant to be extensible at runtime (e.g., a future HR-admin "create custom role" screen) without requiring a schema migration for every new role or permission — unlike the fixed, code-defined enums used elsewhere in the schema (`EmployeeStatus`, `PunchType`, etc.) for values that are true closed sets.

### Decision: `DELETE /:id` means soft-deactivate/terminate everywhere, never a real row delete

All four new modules map the `DELETE` HTTP verb to setting `isActive: false` (departments/designations/work locations) or `status: TERMINATED` + `terminationDate` (employees) — the row is never removed from the table.

Reason: `code-standards.md` and this task both explicitly require never hard-deleting master data that may be linked to attendance/payroll history. Using the `DELETE` verb for a soft action (rather than inventing a separate `POST /:id/deactivate` route) was a deliberate reading of the task's own phrasing ("DELETE or deactivate endpoint") — one endpoint serves both the REST convention and the safety requirement.

### Decision: master-data field names follow the existing schema, not the task's prose

`Designation.title` (not "name"), `WorkLocation.geofenceRadiusMeters` (not "allowedRadiusMeters"), `Employee.hireDate` (not "joiningDate"), `Employee.firstName`/`lastName` (not a single "fullName" column).

Reason: All of these fields already existed from Unit 1 with different names than the task's prose used. Renaming a real, already-migrated column for a purely cosmetic reason isn't worth a migration or the churn of updating every reference to it; the task's intent (what the field represents) is satisfied either way. Where the mismatch is purely presentational (full name), the API response adds a derived field (`fullName`) instead of touching storage.

### Decision: foreign-key existence is checked in the service layer, not left to the database

`EmployeesService.assertReferencesExist()` looks up `departmentId`/`designationId`/`workLocationId` before every write and throws a named `400 Bad Request` if any doesn't exist, rather than letting an invalid ID reach Postgres and surface as a raw `P2003` foreign-key-violation error.

Reason: `code-standards.md` requires clean validation errors and forbids leaking internal details; a raw Prisma/Postgres constraint error is neither clean nor named per-field. The extra lookup queries are cheap relative to the write itself and only run on mutations (not reads), so the cost is proportionate to a payroll-adjacent write path, not the fast, high-volume punch path this doesn't touch.

### Decision: `AttendanceDevice.deviceType` is a new field; `sourceType` stays and is derived from it, not replaced

Added `deviceType` (hardware category: `BIOMETRIC`/`FACE_RECOGNITION`/`CARD`/`QR_TERMINAL`/`TURNSTILE`/`OTHER`) as a new, user-facing registry field. The existing `sourceType` (`AttendanceSourceType`) column is untouched and is now populated automatically from `deviceType` via a fixed mapping in `AttendanceDevicesService`, never exposed in the DTOs.

Reason: `sourceType` already existed as a required column serving a different, established purpose from Unit 1 — tagging raw punches for the (not-yet-built) attendance validation engine, using a shared enum that also covers non-device sources like `MOBILE`/`WEB`. Repurposing or extending it for this unit's hardware-category concept would conflate two different classification axes and risk breaking an invariant `architecture.md` already documents. Deriving one from the other keeps both concerns intact with zero duplicate user input.

### Decision: `POST /:id/test-connection` is a real, audited endpoint that does no networking

Implemented as a genuine guarded/audited route returning a fixed placeholder body, rather than omitted or stubbed as a TODO.

Reason: The task explicitly specified the exact response shape and required it be audited "if implemented as an action" — implementing it properly (with its own permission, `attendanceDevices.testConnection`) now means the real connection-testing logic can be dropped in later without changing the route, permission, or audit contract that any client (web/mobile admin UI) would already depend on.

### Decision: `MobileDevice.osName`/`isActive` are dropped, not kept alongside the new `platform`/`status` fields

Unlike Unit 5's `AttendanceDevice.deviceType`/`sourceType` (kept both, since `sourceType` had an established downstream consumer), `MobileDevice.osName` and `isActive` were removed entirely in the same migration that added `platform` and `status`.

Reason: Neither field had any consumer anywhere in the codebase or `architecture.md` — unlike `sourceType`, which Unit 1's raw-punch design explicitly depends on. Keeping a redundant `isActive: Boolean` alongside the new `status: MobileDeviceStatus` enum would create a real inconsistent-state risk (e.g., `isActive:true` while `status:BLOCKED`) with no corresponding benefit, since `status` alone fully expresses what `isActive` used to track plus three more states it couldn't. The table was provably empty, so dropping was a zero-data-risk migration.

### Decision: primary-device handoff only touches `isPrimary`, never `status`, on the device being demoted

`POST /:id/approve`, when making a device primary, sets `isPrimary: false` on any other primary device for the same employee but leaves its `status` untouched (still `APPROVED` if it was).

Reason: The task's own principle — "every status change must go through an explicit endpoint" — reads as applying to the `MobileDeviceStatus` enum specifically. Auto-blocking or auto-deactivating a *different* device's *status* as a side effect of approving *this* one would violate that principle for the device not being acted on directly. `isPrimary` is a lower-stakes attribute (which approved device is currently trusted for attendance), not a security-status transition, so flipping it automatically is safe and matches the task's explicit instruction to "ensure only one approved primary device per employee." An HR admin who wants to fully retire the old device still has `/block` or `/reset` available as an explicit, separate action.

### Decision: every mobile punch attempt is stored, even for an unknown/mismatched/unapproved device — the only true rejection is "no identifiable employee"

`AttendancePunchesService.createMobile` never throws for a missing, cross-employee, or not-yet-approved `MobileDevice` — it stores a raw punch tagged `REJECTED_UNREGISTERED_DEVICE` with a distinguishing `fraudFlags` entry in every case. The single exception that returns a `400` with nothing stored is when the calling `User` has no linked `employeeId` at all.

Reason: `CLAUDE.md`'s non-negotiable principles state "every punch attempt must be stored, including rejected and suspicious attempts" without qualification — an unrecognized or mismatched device is precisely the kind of suspicious attempt this exists to capture for HR/security review, not discard. The one case that can't be stored meaningfully is when we don't know *who* attempted the punch at all (no employee context whatsoever), which is a fundamentally different failure mode from "we know who, but something about their device is wrong."

### Decision: `fraudScore` is always server-set to `0` at intake; client-reported `fraudSignals` are stored but never scored

`CreateMobilePunchDto.fraudSignals` (an arbitrary client-reported object) is preserved verbatim into `fraudFlags`, but `AttendanceRawPunch.fraudScore` is hardcoded to `0` on every create, never derived from client input.

Reason: `architecture.md`'s invariant is explicit — "mobile app fraud checks are signals; backend validation is authoritative." A numeric fraud score is a trust-bearing, potentially payroll-consequential value; accepting one from an unauthenticated-in-intent client input would let a compromised or modified mobile client simply claim `fraudScore: 0` regardless of reality. No real scoring logic exists yet (that's Unit 8), so `0` (unscored) is the only honest value the server itself can currently assert — the raw signals are kept for whenever real scoring logic is built, not discarded.

### Decision: device-import duplicates return the existing record, not a `409 Conflict`

A repeated `POST /attendance-punches/device-import` with the same `attendanceDeviceId` + `sourceId` returns the original raw punch unchanged (`200 OK`), rather than an error.

Reason: Real device-sync integrations retry on network failure and will legitimately resend the same log entry more than once. `code-standards.md` requires device imports to be idempotent; treating a duplicate as a successful no-op (same resource, same response) rather than an error is what actual idempotency means for a sync client — an error would force every device-import integration to special-case "409 means fine, ignore it," which is worse than just returning the record.

### Decision: `apps/api/tsconfig.json` no longer sets `"declaration": true`

Reason: `apps/api` is a deployed application, not a package anything else in the monorepo imports types from — declaration output had no consumer. It was also the direct cause of a real `TS2742` compile error the moment a controller method's inferred return type included a Prisma `Json`-typed field (this unit's `AttendanceRawPunch.fraudFlags`/`rawPayload` are the first `Json` fields returned by any controller so far). Removing the flag fixes the root cause rather than working around it with per-method type annotations or casts, and does not affect `nest build`'s runtime output (build/typecheck/root-typecheck all re-verified green afterward).

### Decision: `AttendanceRawPunch.validatedAt`/`validatedByUserId` were added, not skipped

Added a small additive migration for these two nullable fields (`validatedAt: DateTime?`, `validatedByUserId: String?` as a real `User` relation) rather than relying solely on `AuditLog` to answer "who validated this and when."

Reason: The task explicitly framed this as a choice to make and document either way. Every other "reviewed" entity in this schema (`MobileDevice.approvedAt`/`approvedByUserId`/`blockedAt`/`blockedByUserId`, from Unit 6) already exposes this exact shape of metadata directly on the row, and a payroll-critical validation outcome is at least as worth querying directly (e.g., "list punches HR validated today") as a mobile-device approval is. The migration is purely additive (two nullable columns + an FK), so it carries no risk to the existing `attendance_raw_punches` data.

### Decision: `fraudScore`/`fraudFlags` are recomputed from scratch on every validation run, not accumulated

Each call to `validatePunch`/`validatePending` computes a full fresh fraud assessment and overwrites `fraudScore` entirely; `fraudFlags` entries tagged `{source: "VALIDATION_ENGINE"}` are replaced wholesale (non-engine, intake-time flags are preserved untouched alongside them).

Reason: The task itself asked for "simple and deterministic" fraud scoring. A running/accumulating score would mean re-validating the same unchanged punch twice produces a different (and misleading) result each time — not idempotent, and not something HR could trust as "the current risk assessment," only "the sum of every assessment ever run." Recomputing from scratch means `fraudScore` always answers "how risky does this punch look right now, given current facts," which is the more useful and less surprising invariant for a payroll-adjacent field.

### Decision: duplicate detection short-circuits before any source-specific validation, and takes priority over device-approval/geofence rejections

`resolveDuplicateOriginal` runs immediately after the common employee-active check, before branching into mobile/device/manual rules. If a duplicate is found (or one was already recorded on a prior run), the punch is marked `REJECTED_DUPLICATE` and none of the source-specific rules run at all.

Reason: A punch that's a duplicate of an already-processed punch doesn't need — and shouldn't get — a second independent geofence/device/GPS assessment; whatever the original punch's fate was is the one that matters, and re-deriving a possibly-different verdict for what is physically the same punch event would be confusing and wasteful. This was confirmed correct (not a bug) during manual verification: a punch from a still-unapproved device, submitted close in time to an already-validated same-type punch, was correctly flagged `REJECTED_DUPLICATE` rather than `REJECTED_UNREGISTERED_DEVICE`, since duplicate detection ran first exactly as designed.

### Decision: manual punches always resolve to `PENDING_MANAGER_APPROVAL`, not `PENDING_HR_REVIEW`

Reason: The task explicitly offered both as options for "manual punches must never be auto-accepted." Unit 7 used `PENDING_HR_REVIEW` uniformly at *intake* time because no validation engine existed yet to make any real distinction. Now that this engine exists, a manually-typed correction is specifically the kind of exception that calls for a manager's sign-off on whether the correction itself is legitimate — `PENDING_MANAGER_APPROVAL` more accurately represents what's actually being asked for than a generic "HR will look at it eventually" queue.

### Decision: Asia/Kuwait is treated as a fixed UTC+3 offset, not a full IANA timezone lookup

`kuwait-date.util.ts` implements `toKuwaitWorkDate`/`kuwaitWorkDateRange` using a hardcoded `+3h` constant rather than a timezone library.

Reason: Kuwait has not observed daylight saving time since 1968 — a fixed offset is not an approximation for this specific timezone, it is exactly correct. Pulling in a full tz library (`luxon`/`date-fns-tz`) for a single, DST-free, company-wide timezone would be speculative infrastructure ahead of actual need. This must be revisited (see open questions) the moment a second, DST-observing site timezone is ever required.

### Decision: `recalculate-range` never creates summaries for employee/days with zero raw punches

Candidate employee/days are discovered strictly from existing `AttendanceRawPunch` rows (any `validationStatus`) within the requested range — never by enumerating all active employees × all calendar days in the range.

Reason: `CLAUDE.md`/this unit's own task text explicitly forbid bulk-creating `ABSENT` records for every employee on every day — that's a distinct, much bigger feature (a real "who didn't punch today" absence-detection sweep) that needs its own deliberate policy decision about leave/week-off/holiday interaction, none of which exists yet. Scoping this unit strictly to "recalculate days that already have punch activity" keeps it small and avoids accidentally becoming the absence-tracking feature.

### Decision: `calculateForEmployeeDate` is the single code path for both the one-shot and batch endpoints

`POST /calculate` and `POST /recalculate-range` both funnel through the exact same `calculateForEmployeeDate` method — the batch endpoint is a discovery-and-looping wrapper around it, not a separate calculation implementation.

Reason: `architecture.md`'s own invariant #13 requires recalculation to be deterministic — same input events and policy version produce the same summary, regardless of *how* the recalculation was triggered. Two independent implementations of the same derivation logic would be a correctness risk (they could quietly drift) and is exactly the kind of duplicated business-rule surface `code-standards.md` warns against. This also means audit granularity (see below) and locked-summary protection are automatically consistent across both endpoints for free, rather than needing to be re-implemented and re-verified twice.

### Decision: audit granularity is per employee/day, not per HTTP call (unlike Unit 8's batch-validate design)

Every individual `calculateForEmployeeDate` invocation — whether called once from `POST /calculate` or many times inside `POST /recalculate-range`'s loop — writes its own `ATTENDANCE_SUMMARY_CALCULATED`/`_RECALCULATED` audit row with a real before/after snapshot.

Reason: Unit 8's single "one row per batch call" design was correct there because both audit actions represented the identical underlying side effect (validate a punch), differing only by which endpoint triggered it. Here, `_CALCULATED` vs. `_RECALCULATED` is a genuine, payroll-relevant distinction per employee/day (first-time result vs. a change to an existing daily attendance record), and `code-standards.md` requires every payroll-critical mutation to be individually auditable — compressing up to 100 real daily-summary changes into one opaque batch entry would make it impossible to answer "what was this specific day's summary before this recalculation" without re-deriving it, which the audit log exists specifically to avoid.

### Decision: `targetPunchId` is a real `AttendanceRawPunch` relation, not a loose string

`AttendanceCorrection.targetPunchId` is modeled with `@relation("CorrectionTargetPunch", ...)` referencing `AttendanceRawPunch`, with `onDelete: SetNull`.

Reason: consistent with every other nullable target-row reference already in this schema (`duplicateOfPunchId`, `officialInPunchId`/`officialOutPunchId`) — a real FK lets Postgres itself guarantee the reference is valid, and lets Prisma `include` it directly in list/detail responses without a second manual lookup. `onDelete: SetNull` (not `Restrict`/`Cascade`) matches the pattern used for `duplicateOfPunchId` — since raw punches are never actually deleted in normal operation, this only matters as a defensive default, not an expected code path.

### Decision: correction requests are always allowed even against an already-locked day; only approval is blocked

Reason: the task explicitly offered this as the "safer" option, and it matches how HR processes actually work — a dispute or missed-punch discovery doesn't stop existing just because payroll already locked the period; what must stop is the *silent* re-opening of a locked, already-processed attendance record. Recording the request (with its full before/after snapshot) preserves the fact that a correction was asked for and why, which is itself useful audit trail even if it can't be approved without first going through a (not-yet-built) reopen/post-lock-correction workflow per `architecture.md`'s Layer 5.

### Decision: `requestedPunchType` is server-derived for 4 of the 6 correction types, never trusted from the client

`MISSED_IN`/`MISSED_OUT`/`WRONG_IN`/`WRONG_OUT` always get their punch type from a fixed `correctionType → PunchType` lookup; only `MANUAL_ADJUSTMENT` asks the client for it.

Reason: `correctionType` and "which punch type this is correcting" are the same fact expressed twice if both come from the client — trusting the client's `requestedPunchType` independently of `correctionType` would let a request claim `WRONG_IN` while actually supplying `OUT`, an inconsistency with no legitimate use case and real potential for confusion in a payroll-adjacent record. Deriving it removes an entire class of client/server disagreement rather than validating against it after the fact.

### Decision: correction approval is two sequential operations (transactional punch+status update, then a separate calculator call), not one atomic transaction

See the matching Open Question above for the tradeoff this accepts. Reason for choosing it anyway: `code-standards.md`'s "recalculate daily summaries through one service/function" rule and `architecture.md`'s invariant #13 ("recalculation must be deterministic... same input events and policy version produce same summary") both argue strongly against maintaining a second, correction-workflow-specific copy of the official-IN/OUT-selection logic just to fit it inside one transaction. A summary is, by design, a derived/recalculable record — a narrow window where it's stale-but-recoverable is a smaller risk than two independently-evolving implementations of the one rule that decides what payroll actually sees quietly drifting apart.

### Decision: session state is a plain React Context, not a state-management library

`lib/auth/auth-context.tsx` uses `React.createContext` + `useState`/`useEffect`/`useCallback` — no zustand/jotai/redux was added.

Reason: the target stack list for this unit (Next.js, TypeScript, Tailwind, shadcn/ui, React Hook Form, Zod, TanStack Query, Lucide) does not name a global state library, and the task explicitly warns against "random UI libraries outside the chosen stack." The session state this foundation needs (current user, loading flag, login/logout actions) is small and doesn't benefit from a dedicated store — plain Context is the standard, zero-dependency React/Next.js App Router pattern for exactly this shape of problem.

### Decision: shadcn/ui primitives were hand-authored, not scaffolded via the `shadcn` CLI

`components/ui/{button,input,label,card}.tsx` were written directly in shadcn's own convention (`cva` for variants, `cn()` for class merging) rather than running `pnpm dlx shadcn@latest add ...`.

Reason: shadcn/ui's own model is "copy this code into your project," not "install a component library" — hand-authoring the handful of primitives this foundation actually needs achieves exactly the same end state the CLI would produce, without a network-dependent interactive scaffolding step (unsuitable for a non-interactive agent environment) and without pulling in Radix-based dependencies for interactive components (dropdown, dialog, popover) that nothing in this foundation unit uses yet. Add the CLI-driven workflow (and its Radix dependencies) when a future unit genuinely needs one of those interactive primitives.

### Decision: Tailwind v4 `@theme` tokens are generated from, not a replacement for, `ui-context.md`'s literal CSS variable names

`app/globals.css` keeps a `:root` block with the exact variable names `ui-context.md` specifies (`--bg-base`, `--text-primary`, etc.) as the source of truth, and a separate `@theme inline` block maps those to clean Tailwind-utility-facing names (`--color-background`, `--color-foreground`, ...) via `var()` references.

Reason: `ui-context.md` is the canonical design-token spec and should stay directly quotable/traceable in the CSS; Tailwind v4's `@theme` naming convention (which drives what utility class names get generated, e.g. `--color-border` → `border-border`) is a separate, tooling-specific concern. Keeping both layers means updating a color in one place (`:root`) automatically updates every utility built on top of it, and nothing in the component layer ever hardcodes a hex value, per `ui-context.md`'s explicit rule.

### Decision: `eslint-config-next`'s natively-flat exports are imported directly, not via `FlatCompat`

`eslint.config.mjs` imports `eslint-config-next/core-web-vitals` and `eslint-config-next/typescript` directly and spreads them into the exported array, rather than using `@eslint/eslintrc`'s `FlatCompat.extends(...)` bridge (the pattern shown in most current Next.js documentation/tutorials).

Reason: this is a real, reproduced bug, not a hypothetical — `eslint-config-next@16.2.10` already ships flat-config-native exports, and passing an already-flat config back through `FlatCompat`'s legacy-schema JSON validator caused a `TypeError: Converting circular structure to JSON` crash on every `lint` invocation. Importing the flat exports directly is both the fix and the simpler, more correct approach for this exact dependency version — root-caused rather than pinned around.

### Decision: `authorizedRequest()` reads the access token from storage directly, rather than threading it through every API call

`lib/api/client.ts`'s `authorizedRequest<T>()` calls `getStoredAccessToken()` internally instead of requiring every one of the 20 new resource functions (and every future one) to accept and forward an explicit token argument.

Reason: with 4 resources × 5 operations already in this single unit (and more to come in Units 13+), requiring the token as an explicit parameter at every call site is repetitive boilerplate with real potential for a call site to forget it or use a stale closed-over value. `token-storage.ts` is already the single, synchronous source of truth for the current token (Unit 11's own design), so reading from it directly inside the API layer is simpler and no less correct than threading it manually. Unit 11's `login`/`refresh`/`logout`/`getCurrentUser` functions keep their explicit-token signatures unchanged, since those calls happen *before* a session exists or are managing the session itself.

### Decision: Departments/Designations/Work Locations/Employees each get their own page-level `useQuery`/`useMutation` calls — no shared custom "resource CRUD" hook

Reason: `@tanstack/react-query` already *is* the shared data-fetching abstraction this unit needed; wrapping `useQuery`/`useMutation` in another custom hook layer on top would be an abstraction with no real benefit, especially since the 4 resources' field sets and forms are all genuinely different (Work Locations has lat/lng/type/radius; Employees needs 3 dropdown sources and two distinct forms). The pattern that *is* shared — pagination/search state, the list/create/edit/deactivate flow shape — was extracted into the reusable `components/admin/*` presentational components instead, which is where the actual duplication lived.

### Decision: Employees get two separate form components (create vs. edit), not one shared form with conditional fields

Reason: `CreateEmployeeDto` and `UpdateEmployeeDto` are different shapes on the backend by design (Unit 4) — `employeeCode`/`hireDate` are write-once, `status` is edit-only and excludes `TERMINATED`. Modeling this as one Zod schema/RHF instance with mode-dependent conditional requiredness (`superRefine` keyed off an external "mode" the schema itself can't see) would have been meaningfully more complex than two small, independently-readable components that each map cleanly to one backend DTO. The three relation dropdowns are shared via a small `RelationSelects` presentational component that takes already-resolved `register()` results as props (not the `form` instance) specifically because a union of two different `UseFormRegister<T>` generics is not callable in TypeScript — confirmed by the actual compiler error, not a stylistic guess.

### Decision: numeric form fields use React Hook Form's `setValueAs`/`valueAsNumber`, not `zod.preprocess`, to handle empty inputs

Reason: `z.preprocess(...)` changes a Zod schema's *input* type (as seen by `zodResolver`) to something wider than its *output* type (as seen by `z.infer`), which broke the `Resolver<TFieldValues>` match against `useForm<WorkLocationFormValues>()` — a genuine, reproduced TypeScript error, not a style preference. Converting empty-string-to-`undefined` (for the optional geofence radius) or relying on the DOM's own `valueAsNumber` (for required lat/lng, combined with an explicit `.refine(!Number.isNaN(...))` check since a plain `.min()/.max()` range check does not reliably reject `NaN`) keeps the schema's input and output types identical and keeps `zodResolver`'s generics happy.

### Decision: query-string boolean filters use a shared `parseQueryBoolean` + `@Transform`, not `@Type(() => Boolean)`

Reason: `@Type(() => Boolean)` (from `class-transformer`) calls the JavaScript `Boolean()` constructor on the raw query-string value, and `Boolean("false")` evaluates to `true` (any non-empty string is truthy) — a genuine, reproduced bug that silently broke every `?flag=false` filter across all 5 list endpoints that used this pattern (Departments, Designations, Work Locations, Attendance Devices, Mobile Devices — Units 4/5/6), completely undetected until this unit's own `isActive`/`isPrimary` filter dropdowns needed to actually work correctly. `@IsBoolean()` still "validated" the wrongly-coerced `true`, which is exactly why the bug went unnoticed for 3 units — validation checked the *type* was a boolean, not that it was the *correct* boolean. The fix (`apps/api/src/common/query-boolean.transform.ts`) is a small, shared, explicitly-tested transform applied via `@Transform(({value}) => parseQueryBoolean(value))`, correctly mapping the strings `"true"`/`"false"` (and pass-through real booleans, for safety) rather than relying on JS's implicit truthiness coercion.

### Decision: "show linked work location" / "view employee relation" are resolved client-side, not by adding backend `include`s

Reason: neither `AttendanceDevicesService` nor `MobileDevicesService` includes its `workLocation`/`employee` relation in list/detail responses (confirmed by reading both services directly, not assumed) — and this unit's task explicitly said "no broad backend changes unless a real blocking issue is found." A missing convenience relation is not a correctness bug (unlike the boolean-filter issue above) — the frontend can resolve the same information itself: an unfiltered work-locations lookup map (small, bounded list) for the Attendance Devices table, and per-unique-employeeId `useQueries` (bounded by page size, not total employee count) for the Mobile Devices table. Both approaches were chosen to match the actual scale characteristics of each list (work locations: small and roughly fixed; employees: potentially 2000+, so only fetch what's actually shown).

### Decision: "Admin Request Correction" is gated on `attendanceCorrections.approve`, not `.request`, following real backend behavior over task-text wording

Reason: Unit 10's own manual verification found and fixed a real permission-escalation bug where gating `POST /attendance-corrections/admin-request` on `.request` let any plain `EMPLOYEE`-role account supply an arbitrary `employeeId` in the request body (since `.request` is also the permission employees hold for their own self-service `/request` endpoint). This unit's task text described gating the admin-request UI action on `.request`, which would have reintroduced the same conceptual exposure in the UI layer even though the backend itself already correctly rejects it. Matching the UI's permission gate to the real, live-verified backend requirement (`.approve`) rather than the task's literal wording keeps the "no action buttons without permission" UX rule honest — a button that's visible but always 403s on click is a worse experience than one that's correctly hidden.

### Decision: raw punches have no edit/delete UI anywhere, by omission not by disabled-button

Reason: `CLAUDE.md`'s non-negotiable principle #4 ("raw punch logs must be immutable except for controlled system metadata") is best enforced in the UI by never rendering the affordance at all, rather than rendering a disabled/greyed-out edit button that would visually suggest editing is a conceptually valid-but-currently-blocked action. The Attendance Punches table's row actions are exactly `View`/`Validate` — nothing else — making the immutability invariant something a user can see at a glance, not just something the backend happens to reject.

### Decision: the correction-request form's conditional fields are validated with Zod `.superRefine()`, mirroring the backend's `@ValidateIf` rules field-for-field

Reason: `request-correction.dto.ts`'s conditional-requiredness rules (`requestedPunchType` only for `MANUAL_ADJUSTMENT`; `requestedPunchTime` required for everything except `OTHER`; `targetPunchId` required only for `WRONG_IN`/`WRONG_OUT`) are real backend business rules, not incidental DTO plumbing — reproducing them client-side via `.superRefine()` (rather than making every field simply optional and letting the backend's `400` be the only feedback) gives immediate, specific inline errors instead of a generic failed-submission round trip, consistent with this project's established "validate at the boundary, but give good UX before that boundary" pattern from Units 12/13's own Zod schemas.

### Decision: `react-hook-form`'s `watch()` is avoided in favor of local `useState` for render-affecting conditional logic

Reason: the first-ever use of `watch()` in this codebase immediately triggered a real ESLint React-Compiler "incompatible library" warning (`watch()`'s returned function cannot be safely memoized), which no prior unit had ever encountered because no prior unit had used `watch()`. Replacing it with a plain `useState` driven by the select's own `onChange` handler (via `register(name, { onChange })`) cleared the warning with no loss of functionality, and is more consistent with this codebase's already-established Unit 12/13 preference for local component state over form-library reactivity hooks whenever a value needs to drive conditional rendering.

### Decision: locked-summary error paths were verified via a direct, temporary `psql` flag flip, not skipped for lack of a lock API

Reason: no payroll-lock endpoint exists yet (a genuinely future, out-of-scope unit), so there was no real API path to put a summary into a locked state to test against. Rather than skip verifying the frontend's locked-summary error-display behavior (a hard requirement of this unit's task), a real test summary — created through the actual API, like all other verification data this unit — had its `lockedAt` column set directly via `psql` purely to exercise the already-implemented backend 409 responses, then immediately cleared. This is a diagnostic simulation of a reachable state, not fabricated business data, and is consistent with how `AttendanceSummariesController`/`AttendanceCorrectionsService` already implement and test this exact check internally (per Units 9/10's own `psql`-driven lock/unlock verification pattern).

### Decision: Expo Router, not React Navigation directly, for `apps/mobile`

Reason: the task explicitly asked for a router choice with documented reasoning. Expo Router's file-based routing maps directly onto this unit's tiny, flat screen set (Login, Home, Profile, two placeholders) with near-zero manual wiring, and its `(app)` route-group convention structurally mirrors the exact protected-layout pattern `apps/web` already established in Unit 11 (a `(app)` route group whose layout gates on auth state and redirects). Reusing the same mental model across web and mobile — a route group whose layout is the auth boundary — was judged more valuable than React Navigation's more manual (but more explicit) `NavigationContainer`/`Stack.Navigator` wiring for a 5-screen shell with no deep navigation requirements.

### Decision: mobile token storage is fully async (`expo-secure-store`), unlike web's synchronous `localStorage` wrapper

Reason: SecureStore (backed by iOS Keychain / Android Keystore) has no synchronous API, unlike `window.localStorage`. This is a genuine, unavoidable structural difference from `apps/web`'s Unit 11 token-storage module, not an inconsistency — every mobile auth-context function that reads/writes tokens (`restoreSession`, `login`, `logout`) must `await` the storage call. No AsyncStorage fallback was needed or used; SecureStore installed and worked without native-module friction on this Windows/Expo setup.

### Decision: `zod`/`@hookform/resolvers` were removed from `apps/mobile`, not force-resolved to a single version

Reason: wiring them into the login form surfaced a genuine, reproduced TypeScript error — `@hookform/resolvers`'s zod-resolver type declarations resolve a different physical `zod` copy inside `apps/mobile`'s dependency graph than inside `apps/web`'s (confirmed via direct `require.resolve` probing, not guessed), tripping zod4's own internal version-brand check. A two-field login form does not need schema validation badly enough to justify a monorepo-wide pnpm resolution fix (e.g. a root `overrides`/`resolutions` entry affecting both `apps/web` and `apps/mobile`) to chase down in a foundation unit — React Hook Form's own built-in `rules` validation covers "required" checks perfectly well. Revisit if a future mobile form's validation needs grow complex enough to make solving the underlying conflict worthwhile.

### Decision: Metro's `disableHierarchicalLookup` must stay off in this pnpm monorepo

Reason: Expo's generic monorepo guide suggests setting `disableHierarchicalLookup: true` alongside custom `watchFolders`/`nodeModulesPaths`, but this was reproduced live to break Metro's ability to resolve a package's own transitive dependencies (`expo-router`'s entry file failed to find `@expo/metro-runtime`, then `@expo/log-box`) — because pnpm's nested `.pnpm` virtual-store symlink structure depends on exactly the walk-up-the-tree resolution that setting disables. Removing it (keeping `watchFolders`/`nodeModulesPaths` as supplementary additions, not the exclusive lookup set) fixed both failures, confirmed by successfully compiling full Android and iOS bundles afterward. Generic Expo monorepo advice is written with npm/Yarn's flatter hoisting in mind and does not transfer directly to pnpm's stricter, symlinked model.

### Decision: no TypeScript path alias for mobile imports (relative imports only)

Reason: `apps/web`'s `@/*` alias works because Next.js's bundler natively honors `tsconfig.json` `paths`. Metro does not — it requires an additional Babel resolver plugin, which this environment had no emulator/device available to runtime-verify. Rather than declare an alias in `tsconfig.json` that type-checks correctly but could plausibly fail to *bundle* at runtime (a worse failure mode than no alias at all, since it would only surface on a real device), every mobile source file uses plain relative imports, confirmed working end-to-end via the live Android/iOS bundle-compile test in this unit's Completed notes.

### Decision: device identifier resolution lives in its own module, separate from `device-info.ts`

Reason: the task explicitly offered either location. `getDeviceInfo()` is a pure, synchronous read of already-available native values with no side effects; resolving a stable identifier is fundamentally different in kind — it's async (SecureStore, and on iOS `getIosIdForVendorAsync()`), and in the fallback case it has a genuine side effect (persisting a generated UUID). Mixing a stateful async operation into what was a simple synchronous getter would have forced `getDeviceInfo()`'s whole contract to change for every existing/future caller. A dedicated `device-identifier.ts` keeps each module's contract simple and honest about what it does.

### Decision: the persisted-UUID fallback is scoped as a last resort, not a primary strategy

Reason: `ANDROID_ID` and IDFV are both platform-native identifiers with well-understood, documented reset conditions (factory reset, signing-key change, vendor-wide uninstall) that HR/IT can reason about; a client-generated UUID has no such platform backing and is trivially reset by clearing app storage or reinstalling. Using it only when the platform-native option is genuinely unavailable (web, or iOS's documented transient-null case) keeps the *common* case resting on a real hardware-backed identifier, with the weaker fallback reserved for edge cases — consistent with `architecture.md`'s invariant #6 that every mobile signal is a hint for backend validation, never proof on its own, regardless of which strategy produced it.

### Decision: a `401` on any Device screen API call triggers the existing `logout()`, not a new session-invalidation mechanism

Reason: Unit 15's `authorizedRequest` already clears SecureStore tokens on a `401`, but nothing previously updated the in-memory `AuthContext` state to match, leaving the UI looking authenticated until the next app restart — a real, latent gap, not something invented for this unit. Rather than build a new global "session invalidated" event/callback system to close it repo-wide (speculative for a codebase with only one screen making authenticated calls so far), this unit's own `401` handling calls the auth context's existing `logout()` directly from the screen that hit the `401` — small, scoped, and immediately effective, with the repo-wide version of this problem flagged as an open question for whenever a second authenticated screen exists.

### Decision: location permission is requested only when the user attempts to punch, never proactively on screen open

Reason: the task explicitly asked for one approach with documented reasoning, offering "on screen open" or "on punch attempt" as the choice. Requesting on screen open would force a native permission dialog on every employee who merely opens the Attendance tab to check their recent punch history or device status, before they've decided to take any action — a worse first impression and a violation of Apple/Google's own contextual-permission-request guidance. Requesting only at the moment of a Check In/Out tap ties the OS prompt directly to the action that needs it, and the screen still shows an accurate (if less detailed) permission status beforehand via a read-only, non-prompting check.

### Decision: the mobile app enforces a 100-meter GPS accuracy pre-check client-side, blocking submission rather than warning-and-continuing

Reason: the task explicitly offered this as the "safer" option for this unit and provided the exact threshold. Blocking submission on poor accuracy avoids generating a raw punch record that both the employee and HR already know is likely to be geofence-rejected, saving a review cycle — but this is purely a UX courtesy, not a security control: the backend's own validation engine (Unit 8) re-derives geofence distance and accuracy independently and would reject an inaccurate punch on its own regardless of what the client pre-checked, so a client that skipped or bypassed this pre-check gains no trust advantage.

### Decision: the mobile device-approval gate blocks the punch buttons client-side even though the backend would still accept (and store) the punch anyway

Reason: confirmed by reading `AttendancePunchesService.createMobile` directly — an unapproved/pending/blocked/deactivated device never causes an HTTP error; the punch is stored with `validationStatus: REJECTED_UNREGISTERED_DEVICE`, matching `CLAUDE.md`'s "every punch attempt must be stored" principle.
