The mobile screen's own gate (disabling Check In/Out unless the device is `APPROVED`) exists purely to prevent the employee from wasting effort on a submission that will predictably be rejected, not to enforce a rule the backend doesn't already enforce itself. If the gate is ever bypassed (a stale cached status, a modified client), the backend's own re-derivation of device status at punch time is still the actual authority — nothing about mobile-side gating is load-bearing for correctness.

### Decision: `@hookform/resolvers`'s missing `zod` dependency is fixed via a pnpm `packageExtensions` entry in `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, not a version pin or a code-level workaround

Reason: the actual defect is in a third-party package's own `package.json` (it imports `zod` without declaring it as a dependency or peerDependency, relying entirely on host-project hoisting), not in anything this project's own code does. pnpm's `packageExtensions` mechanism exists specifically to patch exactly this class of upstream metadata gap — declaring the dependency pnpm should have inferred makes the fix deterministic and workspace-wide, immune to future lockfile churn from unrelated package installs in any other app. A narrower fix (e.g. pinning `@hookform/resolvers` to some other version, or duplicating a zod copy manually into `apps/web/node_modules`) would have treated the symptom in one app while leaving the same landmine live for every other app in the workspace.

### Decision: `formatDateForDisplay` forces UTC; `formatDateTimeForDisplay` does not

Reason: these two utilities format conceptually different kinds of values, and treating them identically would be a real, silent bug. A daily summary's `workDate` is a *labeled calendar day* — the backend already resolved which Kuwait date it represents and stored that as a UTC-midnight instant; redisplaying it in the device's own local timezone could shift the date backward or forward by a day depending on where the device is physically set, even though the underlying value never changes. A punch's `punchTimeSource` or a summary's `officialInTime`/`officialOutTime`, by contrast, are *real moments in time* — an employee should see those in their own device's local clock, which is the whole point of showing a "time" at all. Collapsing both into one date-formatting helper would have been simpler code but silently wrong for one of the two cases.

### Decision: "today's" summary is derived client-side from the same list already fetched for history, not a second API call

Reason: `GET /attendance-summaries/me` already orders results by `workDate: desc`, so today's entry (if it exists) is always the newest row in a list fetched for the Summary History section anyway. Issuing a second, `from`/`to`-filtered request specifically for "today" would be a legitimate alternative (and was verified live to also work correctly), but would mean two network round-trips to serve what is fundamentally one dataset viewed two ways. Deriving the "today" view from data already in hand is simpler and matches this project's established preference for the smallest sufficient implementation over a more "correct-looking" but redundant one.

### Decision: `apps/mobile` navigation uses Expo Router's built-in `Tabs` layout, not a hand-rolled bottom bar or a drawer

Reason: the task explicitly asked for "Expo Router tabs or a clear bottom navigation," and `Tabs` is the framework-native, zero-extra-native-code way to get a permanently visible, standard bottom tab bar — no new native dependency beyond an icon set was needed (the vendored `Tabs` implementation in the installed `expo-router` version needs nothing beyond `react-native` itself). A hand-rolled bottom bar would have meant reimplementing safe-area handling, active-tab styling, and route-to-tab mapping that `Tabs` already provides correctly. A drawer was not considered — the task named exactly four flat, equally-weighted destinations with no natural hierarchy, which is exactly what bottom tabs (not a drawer, typically used for many/nested destinations) are for.

### Decision: the SDK version correction discovered mid-unit was accepted, not reverted

Reason: `npx expo install`'s own compatibility-checking logic — the most authoritative signal available in this environment, short of live access to Expo's release notes — rewrote every Expo-ecosystem dependency to a mutually consistent set (SDK 54-equivalent) when a new package was added. Forcing a revert back to the previously-assumed "SDK 57" versions would mean fighting the tool's own resolution logic to reconstruct a state it no longer considers valid, with no reliable way from inside this session to confirm "57" was ever a real target worth preserving. Every existing mobile screen was re-verified (typecheck, lint, fresh bundle compile for both platforms) against the corrected version set before treating this as safe, rather than assuming compatibility.

### Decision: `POST /users/:id/reset-password` lives in a new model-scoped `UsersModule`, not under `/employees` or `/auth`

Reason: a user account being reset may or may not have a linked employee (an HR/admin account has none), so it doesn't belong under `/employees`. It also isn't a self-service action, so it doesn't belong in `AuthModule` alongside login/refresh/change-password. This is the project's first module organized around the `User` model itself rather than a business domain (Employee, Attendance, etc.) — a deliberate, narrow exception, not a new general pattern; future user-management endpoints (if any) should default back to domain-scoped modules unless they share this same "may have no linked domain entity" property.

### Decision: the password policy is deliberately permissive, and the `Recafco@{employeeCode}` default is treated as the reference case it must pass, not an exception to special-case around

Reason: the task was explicit that a real random-generator-grade policy would defeat the point of an HR-friendly temporary password. `validatePasswordPolicy` requires only 8+ characters, one letter, and one number-or-symbol — `Recafco@MIQ` passes on its letters + `@` alone, no digit required. This was written as the policy's own baseline requirement, not as a special-cased allowlist bypass for one specific string, so any future manually-typed HR override or an employee's own chosen new password is held to the identical, equally permissive bar.

### Decision: `EmployeesService.RELATIONS_INCLUDE` gained a `user` include (Option A) instead of a second per-employee API call (Option B)

Reason: the task explicitly asked to pick the smallest safe change. The Employees table needs to decide, per row, whether to show "Create Login" or "Login account exists" + "Reset Password" — doing that via a second API call per row would mean either N+1 requests or a separate bulk-lookup endpoint that doesn't otherwise exist. Adding one `select`-scoped relation to the existing `RELATIONS_INCLUDE` object (mirroring the existing `department`/`designation`/`workLocation` pattern already there) costs one extra join and is already covered by both `findAll` and `findOne` for free, with no new endpoint and no shape change to the pagination contract.

### Decision: password-change/reset revokes every refresh token for the user, not "all except the current session"

Reason: the task allowed this as an acceptable fallback ("if not easy, document future improvement") when isolating the current session isn't cheap. `AccessTokenPayload` has no `jti`/session id linking it to a specific `RefreshToken` row, so determining "current" would require adding session tracking not otherwise needed yet. Revoking everything is also arguably the safer default after a credential change (forces re-authentication everywhere, including any device that might have observed the old password) — documented as a known simplification, not a silent gap, with the real fix (shared session id across both token types) named as a follow-up.

### Decision: reuse the existing `PayrollLockStatus` enum (`OPEN`/`LOCKED`/`REOPENED`) and existing `reopenedByUserId`/`reopenedAt`/`reopenReason` fields instead of adding a parallel `UNLOCKED`-named set

Reason: the task explicitly said "if existing enum differs, use existing one safely," and the Unit 1 schema already had a semantically-identical status (`REOPENED`) and identically-purposed fields for exactly this "who/when/why was this period reopened" concept. Adding a second set of columns (`unlockedByUserId`/`unlockedAt`/`unlockReason`) purely to match the task's suggested vocabulary would have meant two different names for the same fact, with no functional gain — a real migration cost (and a rename-vs-add decision for later) for zero behavioral benefit. The API's own vocabulary ("unlock") is applied at the DTO/endpoint-naming layer only; the underlying schema keeps its original, working names.

### Decision: `PayrollLockGuardService` lives in its own module, imported by both `AttendanceSummariesModule` and `AttendanceCorrectionsModule`, rather than the guard living inside either of them

Reason: both the summary calculator and the corrections-approval flow need the identical period-lock check, and `AttendanceCorrectionsModule` already imports `AttendanceSummariesModule` for an unrelated reason (recalculating a summary after a correction is approved). Putting the guard inside `AttendanceSummariesModule` would work today but would make the dependency directionality confusing (corrections depending on summaries' internals for something conceptually orthogonal to "calculate a summary"). A small, single-purpose `PayrollLocksModule` that only touches `DatabaseService` avoids any circular-import risk (neither summaries nor corrections modules are imported back by it) and keeps the guard's responsibility named and scoped exactly to what it does.

### Decision: requiring `status === LOCKED` before `unlockPeriod()` proceeds *is* the "only if this lock is the active locked period" check — no separate lookup was added

Reason: the overlap-prevention invariant enforced at `create()`/`lockPeriod()` guarantees at most one `LOCKED` period can ever cover a given date range at a time. Given that invariant, a lock row's own `status` field already tells you unambiguously whether *it* is the one currently freezing its date range — a second "is this really the active lock for these dates" query would be redundant work re-deriving a fact the status field already encodes, not a stronger safety check.

### Decision: web admin/employee navigation is decided by `isAdminUser()` — "holds any permission an existing admin nav item requires" — not a role-name check

Reason: the backend's RBAC is the single source of truth for what a user can do, and roles are just a bundle of permissions (a role could theoretically be renamed or restructured without this logic breaking, since it never hardcodes `'EMPLOYEE'` anywhere). Deriving `ADMIN_NAV_PERMISSIONS` directly from the existing `NAV_ITEMS` array (rather than hand-maintaining a second parallel list) also means the two can never silently drift apart — adding a new permission-gated admin nav item automatically extends what counts as "admin" for this check too.

### Decision: new employee web routes (`/device`, `/attendance`, `/corrections`, `/profile`) reuse already-existing "own data" API client functions with zero backend changes

Reason: every one of `getMyMobileDevices`, `listMyAttendancePunches`, `listMyAttendanceSummaries`, `listMyAttendanceCorrections`, and `requestAttendanceCorrection` already existed in `apps/web/lib/api/*.ts`, several explicitly commented as "kept for reuse by a future self-service page" back when they were added purely for the admin screens. This unit is exactly that anticipated reuse — confirming the task's own "no backend business logic unless a real blocking issue is found" constraint required no exception at all, since the backend contract this UI needed was already complete.

### Decision: Expo Router's implicit-tab behavior is now explicitly documented in code, not just fixed once

Reason: the broken extra tab happened because a new screen file was added (Unit 19.2's `change-password.tsx`) without anyone realizing Expo Router auto-registers *any* undeclared route file in a `Tabs` directory as a bottom tab. The fix (`options={{ href: null }}`) is a one-line change, but the underlying rule is easy to trip over again the next time a non-tab screen is added under `apps/mobile/app/(app)/`. A code comment was added at the fix site rather than only fixing it silently, so a future unit adding another pushed-but-not-tabbed screen sees the warning in place before making the same mistake.

### Decision: the benchmark app's shape was borrowed, not its visuals, branding, or backend assumptions

Reason: the task was explicit that RECAFCO should "feel familiar... not copy Pinnacle exactly." Every new screen/component uses this project's own existing color tokens (`src/lib/theme.ts`), its own established card/section styling, and "RECAFCO HRMS" as the fixed brand title everywhere — never the benchmark's name or logo. Where the benchmark implies a backend RECAFCO doesn't have (leave balances, payslips, a directory, draft workflow items), those are rendered as honest, permanently-inert "coming soon" placeholders rather than approximated with fake data or a partial backend — matching this unit's own explicit "no fake leave/document/directory data" hard requirement.

### Decision: Device, Profile, and Corrections moved to `href: null`, not deleted or rebuilt

Reason: all three screens (Units 16, 19.2/19.3, 19 respectively) are fully working, already-verified features with real backend integrations (device registration, profile display, correction requests). The task's own goal was navigation restructuring, not a rewrite — removing them from the tab bar via the same `href: null` mechanism already established for `change-password` in Unit 19.2 achieves the requested bottom-nav shape with zero risk to their internal logic, which was not touched at all.

### Decision: the quick-create sheet and side menu duplicate some destinations (Device, Change Password) rather than being the single source of truth for them

Reason: the task explicitly requires Device to be reachable from "side menu, center + modal, home device status card" and Corrections from "Workflow screen, center + modal, Home Corrections card" — multiple entry points to the same screen is the actual specified design, not an accidental duplication. Each entry point is a one-line `router.push`, not a re-implementation, so there's no logic duplicated, only navigation affordances.

### Decision: the employee sidebar's nav data model was restructured from a flat list to a typed link/group/coming-soon union, not bolted on with booleans

Reason: the Pinnacle benchmark's Workflow/Time Management/Reports grouping genuinely doesn't fit the existing flat `NavItem[]` shape `NAV_ITEMS` (admin) still correctly uses. Rather than overload `NavItem` with optional `children`/`comingSoon` fields that would only ever be populated for the employee case (and leave the admin list carrying unused shape), a separate `EmployeeNavEntry` union (`EmployeeNavLink | EmployeeNavGroup`, with `EmployeeNavGroup.children` itself a `EmployeeNavLink | EmployeeNavComingSoon` union) keeps each nav mode's data shape honest about what it actually needs. `NAV_ITEMS`/`AppSidebar`'s admin rendering path was not touched at all.

### Decision: `AppHeader` branches into two fully separate components (`AdminHeader`/`EmployeeHeader`) rather than one component with conditional JSX throughout

Reason: the task's hard requirement was "keep admin header behavior working" — the safest way to guarantee that literally is to leave the admin rendering path as an unmodified, self-contained function extracted verbatim from the pre-existing `AppHeader`, so there is no shared conditional logic that a future employee-header change could accidentally perturb. The two headers happen to share the `ChangePasswordModal` component (already a stable, unit-tested piece from Unit 19.2), but nothing else.

### Decision: `processNewPunch`/`recalculateIfAccepted` is a shared orchestration layer, not duplicated per intake path

Reason: mobile, device-import, and (harmlessly) manual intake all need the identical "validate now, then recalculate the daily summary if accepted, respecting payroll locks" sequence, and the single existing human-triggered `/validate` endpoint needed the same recalculation behavior added to it too. A shared private `recalculateIfAccepted` (parameterized only by whether the caller is automated, for audit-actor purposes) avoids three near-identical copies of "check lock, call the calculator, handle `ConflictException`, audit the result" that would otherwise drift independently the next time this logic needs a change.

### Decision: automated validation/rejection decisions audit with `actorUserId: null`, even though a real request actor is always available

Reason: the request actor (the employee submitting a mobile punch, or the admin running a device-import/batch-validate) is a real, always-present value, and is used as-is for `calculateForEmployeeDate`'s own required parameter and for every pre-existing, untouched audit action (`ATTENDANCE_PUNCH_MOBILE_CREATED`, `ATTENDANCE_PUNCH_VALIDATED`, etc. — a human genuinely did trigger those). But the three new "AUTO_*" audit actions record a *decision*, not a submission — marking `actorUserId: null` specifically for that decision (plus an explicit `automated: true` in the recalculation audit's snapshot) distinguishes "the engine decided this" from "a human clicked validate," which matters for any future audit-log UI that wants to show who is accountable for a given attendance-status change.

### Decision: `processNewPunch`/`validatePunch` re-fetch the punch from the database whenever recalculation touched it, rather than hand-assembling the expected final state in memory

Reason: a real staleness bug was found live during this unit's own manual verification (see the Unit 20.1 Completed entry) — the summary calculator flips `isOfficial` on the winning punch in its own separate transaction, and `flagLockedPeriodPunch` appends a fraud flag in its own separate `update`, both after the orchestrating method's own in-memory `updated` snapshot was captured. Re-deriving those side effects by hand in the orchestrating method would require it to know the summary calculator's and lock-flagging logic's exact internal behavior, duplicating knowledge that already lives correctly in the database — a single `findUniqueOrThrow` re-fetch is simpler and can never drift out of sync with what those other services actually did.

### Decision: native Expo splash stays image-only; branding text lives in a separate JS-rendered loading screen, not the native splash itself

Reason: Expo's native splash screen (config-plugin driven, shown before any JS executes) only supports a single background color plus one centered image — there is no supported way to add "RECAFCO HRMS" / "Employee Self Service" text to it without baking that text into the image asset itself, which risks looking distorted or off-brand at different screen densities and was explicitly the kind of "AI-looking"/heavy-effort design the task warned against. Since the app already has a second, unavoidable branded-loading moment immediately after (the `isLoading` gate in `(app)/_layout.tsx` while SecureStore/session restoration runs), that JS-rendered screen — which *can* render arbitrary text — is the correct, and only, place for the fuller "RECAFCO HRMS / Employee Self Service / Loading..." branding. No `SplashScreen.preventAutoHideAsync()`/`hideAsync()` calls were added to manually bridge the two, since the config plugin's default auto-hide-on-first-render already produces a clean handoff for an app with no fonts/assets that must preload first.

### Decision: reused the existing `assets/icon.png` for the splash image rather than generating a new dedicated splash asset

Reason: the task's own instruction was to reuse an existing logo file if one exists and is suitable; this one already is — a 3375×3375 square PNG with a plain white background, meaning it needs no cropping/padding to look correct centered on a white `#FFFFFF` splash background with `resizeMode: "contain"`. Creating a second, separate `splash-icon.png` purely to have a differently-named file would have been duplication with no visual benefit, since the exact same pixels would result either way.

## Session Notes

- Employee count corrected to 2000+ employees. Architecture and performance targets must be sized for 2000+ active employees, multi-office punching devices, and peak attendance windows.

- The system must be better than the current third-party ESS while staying safe.
- Current third-party system supports dashboard, leave, attendance request, geo punching, documents, payslip, and workflow requests.
- The new HRMS should not replace the current system immediately.
- Run parallel with the current system for at least 1–2 months before payroll dependency.
- Focus first on attendance accuracy, HR usability, mobile experience, and audit safety.
- Full payroll engine, recruitment, advanced appraisal, and AI face recognition are not first-version priorities.

- A real super admin account now exists in the local `recafco_hrms_dev` database (username from `apps/api/.env`'s `BOOTSTRAP_ADMIN_USERNAME`, currently `superadmin`) with `mustChangePassword: true`. There is no "change password" endpoint yet (out of scope for Unit 3) — needed before this account can be handed to an actual person.

- Unit 4 manual verification created (and then fully deleted) one department, one designation, one work location, one employee, and one temporary `EMPLOYEE`-role test user to exercise the new CRUD/permission/audit flows end-to-end. `recafco_hrms_dev` was confirmed empty of all of these afterward — `employees`/`departments`/`designations`/`work_locations` all have 0 rows, `users` has exactly the one `superadmin` bootstrap account. No demo data was left behind.

- Unit 5 manual verification created (and then fully deleted) one work location and one attendance device to exercise the device registry end-to-end, plus reused the same temporary `EMPLOYEE`-role test-user pattern from Unit 4 (created, tested against, then deleted). `recafco_hrms_dev` was confirmed empty of all master data afterward (`attendance_devices`/`work_locations`/`departments`/`designations`/`employees` all 0 rows); `users` has exactly the one `superadmin` bootstrap account. Note: the `audit_logs` table does retain entries from Unit 4's now-deleted test work location, since audit logs are correctly append-only/immutable even after the audited entity is later removed — this is expected behavior, not leftover demo data in the operational tables.

- Unit 6 manual verification created two full sets of test master data (department/designation/work-location/employee ×2, since testing cross-employee device-conflict rejection genuinely needs two distinct employees), two temporary `EMPLOYEE`-role test users linked via `User.employeeId`, and two mobile devices — all deleted afterward. `recafco_hrms_dev` was confirmed empty of all of these (`mobile_devices`/`employees`/`departments`/`designations`/`work_locations`/`attendance_devices` all 0 rows); `users` has exactly the one `superadmin` bootstrap account. As with Unit 5, `audit_logs` retains historical entries from this and prior units' now-deleted test data — expected, correct append-only behavior, not leftover operational demo data.

- Unit 7 manual verification created one set of test master data (department/designation/work-location/employee/attendance-device), one temporary `EMPLOYEE`-role test user, one mobile device, and 4 raw attendance punches (2 mobile, 1 device-import, 1 manual) — all deleted afterward. `recafco_hrms_dev` was confirmed empty of all of these (`attendance_raw_punches`/`mobile_devices`/`employees`/`attendance_devices`/`departments`/`designations`/`work_locations` all 0 rows); `users` has exactly the one `superadmin` bootstrap account. As with Units 5–6, `audit_logs` retains historical entries from prior units' now-deleted test data — expected, correct append-only behavior.

- Unit 8 manual verification created one set of test master data (department/designation/geofenced work location/employee/attendance-device), one temporary `EMPLOYEE`-role test user, two mobile devices (one approved, one left pending), and 7 raw attendance punches exercising every validation rule path (in-geofence accept, out-of-geofence reject, unapproved-device reject, device-import accept, manual-stays-pending, and a genuine duplicate reject) — all deleted afterward, including the throwaway `tmp-create-test-user.ts` script used to link the test user to its `User` account (no admin user-creation endpoint exists yet, same gap noted since Unit 6). `recafco_hrms_dev` was confirmed empty of all of these afterward (`attendance_raw_punches`/`mobile_devices`/`attendance_devices`/`employees`/`departments`/`designations`/`work_locations` all 0 rows); `users` has exactly the one `superadmin` bootstrap account. As with prior units, `audit_logs` retains historical entries from this and earlier units' now-deleted test data — expected, correct append-only behavior.

- Unit 9 manual verification created one set of test master data (department/designation/geofenced work location/employee/attendance-device), one temporary `EMPLOYEE`-role test user, one mobile device, 7 raw attendance punches across 3 workdates (mixed mobile/device sources, plus deliberately-non-official extras), one `Shift`/`ShiftAssignment` pair inserted directly via `psql` (no shift admin API exists yet), and 3 `AttendanceDailySummary` rows — all deleted afterward (the one summary that was deliberately locked mid-verification was unlocked first, purely so it could be cleaned up like any other test row), including the throwaway `tmp-create-test-user.ts` script. `recafco_hrms_dev` was confirmed empty of all of these afterward (`attendance_daily_summaries`/`attendance_raw_punches`/`shift_assignments`/`shifts`/`mobile_devices`/`attendance_devices`/`employees`/`departments`/`designations`/`work_locations` all 0 rows); `users` has exactly the one `superadmin` bootstrap account. As with prior units, `audit_logs` retains historical entries from this and earlier units' now-deleted test data — expected, correct append-only behavior.

- Unit 10 manual verification created one set of test master data (department/designation/geofenced work location/employee), one temporary `EMPLOYEE`-role test user, one mobile device, 1 raw punch (mobile `IN`) plus 1 manual `OUT` punch created by an approved correction, 1 `AttendanceDailySummary` (deliberately locked and then unlocked mid-verification, same purely-for-testing pattern as Unit 9), and 5 `AttendanceCorrection` rows (one `MISSED_OUT` approved, one `WRONG_IN` rejected, one `MANUAL_ADJUSTMENT` left `PENDING` to test the locked-summary block, one `OTHER` created by the exploited-then-fixed permission bug, one `OTHER` created legitimately post-fix) — all deleted afterward, including the throwaway `tmp-create-test-user.ts` script. `recafco_hrms_dev` was confirmed empty of all of these afterward (`attendance_corrections`/`attendance_daily_summaries`/`attendance_raw_punches`/`mobile_devices`/`employees`/`departments`/`designations`/`work_locations` all 0 rows); `users` has exactly the one `superadmin` bootstrap account. As with prior units, `audit_logs` retains historical entries from this and earlier units' now-deleted test data — expected, correct append-only behavior.

- Unit 11 manual verification created **no database rows at all** — this unit only exercised the existing `/auth/*` endpoints against the already-bootstrapped `superadmin` account (login, `/auth/me`, refresh-rotation, logout, invalid-token rejection), so there was nothing to clean up afterward. Port 3000 was already occupied on this development machine by the unrelated, pre-existing `recafco-maintenance-management-system` Next.js dev server — confirmed via `Get-NetTCPConnection`/process inspection *before* taking any action, then the web app's own dev server was run on port 3001 purely for this verification session (the package's actual configured `dev` script is unchanged and still targets port 3000). Only this session's own API (port 4000) and web (port 3001) processes were stopped afterward; the unrelated maintenance-app processes were confirmed still running, untouched, both before and after.

- Unit 12 manual verification created, then fully deleted: 2 departments, 2 designations, 2 work locations, and 1 employee (department/designation/work-location's second copies existed specifically to give the employee real FK targets while its first copies exercised the plain deactivate flow) — no test users were needed this time since only the existing bootstrapped `superadmin` account performed every action. Rather than assume the original stop-the-dev-server approval implicitly covered this newly-discovered PID, a second, specific confirmation was sought before stopping it too — the auto-mode safety classifier itself had already blocked one attempt to stop a broader, insufficiently-disclosed process set earlier in this unit, reinforcing that each additional PID needs its own explicit disclosure, not a blanket "restart the dev server" authorization. - Unit 20.1 manual verification created, then fully deleted: 1 geofenced work location (`U201HQ`), 1 employee (`U201TEST`) and its provisioned login (taken through a full mobile-device lifecycle — registered, approved, blocked, reset, re-approved, to exercise Tests E and the `DEVICE_BLOCKED`/`DEVICE_DEACTIVATED` flag paths), 1 attendance device (`U201DEV01`, for Test C's device-import/mixed-source checks), 2 payroll-lock periods (each taken `OPEN` → `LOCKED`, for Test H, on two different dates since the first was needed again after the locked-flag staleness fix required a clean re-test), 16 raw attendance punches spanning all 9 lettered test cases (A–I), and 4 daily summaries recalculated from them. Confirmed via `psql` that every one of these rows, plus their `user_roles`/`refresh_tokens` rows, are gone, and that `MIQ`/`superadmin` (pre-existing, not created by any session) remain the only employee/user rows. **A genuine cleanup-tooling gotcha was hit and worked around**: the first cleanup attempt ran all 8 deletes as one `psql -c "...; ...; ..."` invocation, and when a later statement hit an undisclosed FK constraint (`user_roles`/`refresh_tokens` rows created by intermediate re-login/reset-password calls during verification, not just the original creation), the *entire batch* silently rolled back — including the deletes that had already reported success — because `psql -c` sends multiple `;`-separated statements as one implicit transaction. Diagnosed by re-querying row counts after the "successful" first attempt and finding them unchanged, then fixed by re-running each delete as its own separate `psql -c` invocation (its own transaction) in strict FK-safe order. The already-running API dev server (left over from earlier in this same long session, confirmed still healthy via `/health`) picked up this unit's two rounds of code changes (the initial implementation, then the `isOfficial`/locked-flag staleness fix found during verification) via hot-reload without ever needing a restart, since no schema/migration change was needed this unit — the first unit since Unit 7 with genuinely zero schema changes. Neither the web dev server nor Expo/Metro was touched, since this unit made no `apps/web`/`apps/mobile` UI changes (only `apps/web/lib/api/attendance-punches.ts`'s type definitions, verified via typecheck/build, not a running browser).

- Unit 19.6 manual verification created **no database rows at all** — pure `apps/mobile` branding/config work with zero backend interaction, so there was nothing to clean up in `recafco_hrms_dev`. **The pre-existing Metro/Expo dev server (left running from earlier in this session, on port 8081) died during this unit's own `expo install expo-splash-screen` step** — confirmed gone entirely (no process, no listener), not just unresponsive, most likely from its file-watcher holding a stale view of `node_modules` across the dependency install. A first replacement instance (also port 8081, no `--clear`) came up but every bundle request 404'd with `Unable to resolve module ./index`; investigation traced this to `metro.config.js`'s `watchFolders = [workspaceRoot]` making the HTTP bundle endpoint resolve relative to the **workspace root**, not `apps/mobile` — meaning the plain `/index.bundle?platform=...` URL this project's every prior mobile unit used for verification was never actually the literal request path; the *real* path (only discoverable by fetching the manifest, `GET /` with a browser/device request) is the fully-resolved `node_modules/.pnpm/expo-router@<hash>/.../entry.bundle` location. A second, cleanly-`--clear`ed instance on port 8082 was started, and using the manifest-derived path, both Android and iOS bundles compiled successfully (`200`, ~7.36–7.37MB) and were grepped to confirm the new `BrandedLoadingScreen` component/text plus every pre-existing screen string, confirming no regression. **Both Metro/Expo instances started by this unit (the port-8081 replacement and the port-8082 clean instance) were stopped at the end of verification** — this session was responsible for their lifecycle once the original, pre-existing instance died as a side effect of this unit's own dependency install; no mobile dev server is left running as of the end of this unit. The API and web dev servers were not touched at any point (no backend/web code changed).
